</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Justin Pierre James Trudeau</div><br /><span style="display:none"> (<span class="bday">1971-12-25</span>) </span>December 25, 1971<span class="noprint ForceAgeToShow"> (age&#160;51)</span><br /><a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a>, Canada</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>

<p><b>Justin Pierre James Trudeau</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">MP</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/uː/: &#39;oo&#39; in &#39;goose&#39;">uː</span><span title="&#39;d&#39; in &#39;dye&#39;">d</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/uː/: &#39;oo&#39; in &#39;goose&#39;">uː</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;d&#39; in &#39;dye&#39;">d</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="ext-phonos"><span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/en\/transcoded\/e\/ea\/Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg\/Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/transcoded/e/ea/Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg/Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" title="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt navigation-not-searchable"><a href="/wiki/File:Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg" title="File:Justinpierrejamestrudeau.ogg">ⓘ</a></sup></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">TROO</span>-doh, troo-<span style="font-size:90%">DOH</span></i></a>, <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small">French:</span> <span class="IPA nowrap" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">&#91;ʒystɛ̃<span class="wrap"> </span>pjɛʁ<span class="wrap"> </span>dʒɛms<span class="wrap"> </span>tʁydo&#93;</a></span>; born December 25, 1971) is a Canadian politician who has served as the 23rd <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> since 2015 and the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party</a> since 2013. 
</p><p>Trudeau was born in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> and attended <a href="/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_Jean-de-Br%C3%A9beuf" title="Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf">Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf</a>. He graduated from <a href="/wiki/McGill_University" title="McGill University">McGill University</a> in 1994 with a Bachelor of Arts degree in literature, then in 1998 acquired a Bachelor of Education degree from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia" title="University of British Columbia">University of British Columbia</a>. After graduating he taught at the <a href="/wiki/Secondary_school" title="Secondary school">secondary school</a> level in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, before relocating back to Montreal in 2002 to further his studies. He was chair for the youth charity <a href="/wiki/Katimavik" title="Katimavik">Katimavik</a> and director of the not-for-profit <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Avalanche_Association" title="Canadian Avalanche Association">Canadian Avalanche Association</a>. In 2006, he was appointed as chair of the Liberal Party's Task Force on Youth Renewal.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_election" title="2008 Canadian federal election">2008 federal election</a>, he was elected to represent the riding of <a href="/wiki/Papineau_(electoral_district)" title="Papineau (electoral district)">Papineau</a> in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>. He was the Liberal Party's <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official Opposition critic</a> for youth and multiculturalism in 2009, and the following year he became critic for citizenship and immigration. In 2011, he was appointed as a critic for secondary education and sport. Trudeau won the <a href="/wiki/2013_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2013 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership of the Liberal Party</a> in April 2013 and led his party to victory in the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 federal election</a>, moving the third-placed Liberals from 36 seats to 184 seats, the largest-ever numerical increase by a party in a Canadian federal election. Trudeau is the second-youngest prime minister in Canadian history after <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>; he is also the first to be the child of a previous holder of the post, as the eldest son of <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>.
</p><p>Major government initiatives he undertook during his first term as prime minister included legalizing recreational marijuana through the <i><a href="/wiki/Cannabis_Act" title="Cannabis Act">Cannabis Act</a></i>; attempting Senate appointment reform by establishing the <a href="/wiki/Independent_Advisory_Board_for_Senate_Appointments" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent Advisory Board for Senate Appointments">Independent Advisory Board for Senate Appointments</a> and establishing the <a href="/wiki/Greenhouse_Gas_Pollution_Pricing_Act" title="Greenhouse Gas Pollution Pricing Act">federal carbon tax</a>. In foreign policy, Trudeau's government negotiated trade deals such as the <a href="/wiki/Canada-United_States-Mexico_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada-United States-Mexico Agreement">Canada-United States-Mexico Agreement</a> (CUSMA) and the <a href="/wiki/Comprehensive_and_Progressive_Agreement_for_Trans-Pacific_Partnership" title="Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership">Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership</a>, and signed the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Agreement" title="Paris Agreement">Paris Agreement</a> on climate change. He was sanctioned by Canada's <a href="/wiki/Ethics_Commissioner_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethics Commissioner of Canada">ethics commissioner</a> for violating <a href="/wiki/Conflict_of_interest" title="Conflict of interest">conflict of interest</a> rules regarding the <a href="/wiki/Aga_Khan_affair" title="Aga Khan affair">Aga Khan affair</a>, and later again with the <a href="/wiki/SNC-Lavalin_affair" title="SNC-Lavalin affair">SNC-Lavalin affair</a>.
</p><p>Trudeau led the Liberals to a <a href="/wiki/Minority_governments_in_Canada" title="Minority governments in Canada">minority government</a> victory in the <a href="/wiki/2019_Canadian_federal_election" title="2019 Canadian federal election">2019 federal election</a>. During his second term, his government responded to the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">COVID-19 pandemic</a>, announced an <a href="/wiki/Firearms_regulation_in_Canada" title="Firearms regulation in Canada">"assault-style" weapons ban</a> in response to the <a href="/wiki/2020_Nova_Scotia_attacks" title="2020 Nova Scotia attacks">2020 Nova Scotia attacks</a>, and was investigated for a third time by the ethics commissioner for his part in the <a href="/wiki/WE_Charity_scandal" title="WE Charity scandal">WE Charity scandal</a>, but was cleared of wrongdoing. In the <a href="/wiki/2021_Canadian_federal_election" title="2021 Canadian federal election">2021 federal election</a>, he led the Liberals to another minority government. 
</p><p>During his third term, he invoked the <i><a href="/wiki/Emergencies_Act" title="Emergencies Act">Emergencies Act</a></i> in response to the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_Convoy_2022" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom Convoy 2022">Freedom Convoy protests</a> (the first time the act was brought into force since it was enacted in 1988) and responded to the <a href="/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine" title="Russian invasion of Ukraine">Russian invasion of Ukraine</a> by imposing sanctions on Russia and authorizing military aid to Ukraine.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ancestry_and_birth">Ancestry and birth</span></h3>
<p>On June 23, 1971, the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_the_Prime_Minister_(Canada)" title="Office of the Prime Minister (Canada)">Prime Minister's Office</a> (PMO) announced that Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>'s wife of four months, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Trudeau" title="Margaret Trudeau">Margaret Trudeau</a> (née Sinclair),<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> was pregnant and due in December.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Justin Trudeau was born on December 25, 1971, at 9:27&#160;pm <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Time_Zone" title="Eastern Time Zone">EST</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Civic_Hospital" title="Ottawa Civic Hospital">Ottawa Civic Hospital</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He is the second child in Canadian history to be born to a prime minister in office; the first was <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>'s daughter Margaret Mary Theodora Macdonald (February 8, 1869&#160;– January 28, 1933). Trudeau's younger brothers <a href="/wiki/Alexandre_Trudeau" title="Alexandre Trudeau">Alexandre</a> (Sacha) (born December 25, 1973) and <a href="/wiki/Michel_Trudeau" title="Michel Trudeau">Michel</a> (October 2, 1975&#160;– November 13, 1998) were the third and fourth.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-English2007_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-English2007-12">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau is predominantly of <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish Canadian">Scottish</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="French Canadian">French Canadian</a> descent.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His grandfathers were businessman <a href="/wiki/Charles-%C3%89mile_Trudeau" title="Charles-Émile Trudeau">Charles-Émile Trudeau</a><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and Scottish-born <a href="/wiki/James_Sinclair_(politician)" title="James Sinclair (politician)">James Sinclair</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-surprised_wedding_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-surprised_wedding-15">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> who was <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Fisheries_and_Oceans_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Fisheries and Oceans (Canada)">minister of fisheries</a> in the cabinet of Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau's maternal great-grandfather Thomas Bernard was born in <a href="/wiki/Makassar" title="Makassar">Makassar</a>, Indonesia<sup id="cite_ref-mother_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mother-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and immigrated to <a href="/wiki/Penticton" title="Penticton">Penticton, British Columbia</a>, in 1906 at age 15 with his family.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Through the Bernard family, kinsmen of the <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Bandon" title="Earl of Bandon">Earls of Bandon</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau is the fifth great-grandson of Major-General <a href="/wiki/William_Farquhar" title="William Farquhar">William Farquhar</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> a leader in the <a href="/wiki/Founding_years_of_modern_Singapore" title="Founding years of modern Singapore">founding of modern Singapore</a>; Trudeau also has remote <a href="/wiki/Malays_(ethnic_group)" title="Malays (ethnic group)">ethnic Malaccan</a><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Nias_people" title="Nias people">Nias</a><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> ancestry.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Margaret_Sinclair,_Pat_Nixon,_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Margaret_Sinclair%2C_Pat_Nixon%2C_Justin_Trudeau_1972-04-14_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1906" data-file-height="1488" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Margaret_Trudeau" title="Margaret Trudeau">Margaret Trudeau</a> with <a href="/wiki/Pat_Nixon" title="Pat Nixon">Pat Nixon</a> holding Justin at <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Hall" title="Rideau Hall">Rideau Hall</a> in Ottawa in 1972.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau was <a href="/wiki/Infant_baptism#Roman_Catholic_Church" title="Infant baptism">baptized</a> with his father's niece Anne Rouleau-Danis as godmother and his mother's brother-in-law Thomas Walker as godfather,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> at <a href="/wiki/Notre-Dame_Cathedral_Basilica_(Ottawa)" class="mw-redirect" title="Notre-Dame Cathedral Basilica (Ottawa)">Ottawa's Notre Dame Basilica</a> on the afternoon of January 16, 1972, which marked his first public appearance.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> and given the names "Justin Pierre James".<sup id="cite_ref-Grafstein_2019_p._1-PT102_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grafstein_2019_p._1-PT102-31">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> On April 14, 1972, Trudeau's father and mother hosted a gala at the <a href="/wiki/National_Arts_Centre" title="National Arts Centre">National Arts Centre</a>, at which visiting U.S. president <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Richard Nixon</a> said, "I'd like to toast the future prime minister of Canada, to Justin Pierre Trudeau" to which Pierre Trudeau responded that should his son ever assume the role, he hoped he would have "the grace and skill of the president".<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Earlier that day first lady <a href="/wiki/Pat_Nixon" title="Pat Nixon">Pat Nixon</a> had visited him in his nursery and gifted him a <a href="/wiki/Stuffed_toy" title="Stuffed toy">stuffed toy</a> <a href="/wiki/Snoopy" title="Snoopy">Snoopy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span></h3>
<p>Trudeau's parents announced their separation in 1977, when he was five years old; his father was given primary custody.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> There were repeated rumours of a reconciliation for many years afterwards.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> However his mother eventually filed for a <a href="/wiki/No-fault_divorce" title="No-fault divorce">no-fault divorce</a> which the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Ontario" title="Supreme Court of Ontario">Supreme Court of Ontario</a> granted in 1984;<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> his father had announced his intention to retire as prime minister a month earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-WoodgerBurg2006_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WoodgerBurg2006-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Eventually his parents came to an amicable joint-custody arrangement and learned to get along quite well. Interviewed in October 1979, his <a href="/wiki/Nanny" title="Nanny">nanny</a> Dianne Lavergne was quoted, "Justin is a mommy's boy, so it's not easy, but children's hurts mend very quickly. And they're lucky kids, anyway."<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Of his mother and father's marriage, Trudeau said in 2009, "They loved each other incredibly, passionately, completely. But there was 30 years between them, and my mom never was an equal partner in what encompassed my father's life, his duty, his country."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau has three half-siblings, Kyle and Alicia, from his mother's remarriage to Fried Kemper,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> and Sarah, from his father's relationship with <a href="/wiki/Deborah_Coyne" title="Deborah Coyne">Deborah Coyne</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau lived at <a href="/wiki/24_Sussex_Drive" title="24 Sussex Drive">24 Sussex Drive</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>, the official residence of Canada's prime minister, from his birth until his father's government was defeated in the <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">1979 federal election</a>. The Trudeaus were expected to move into <a href="/wiki/Stornoway_(residence)" title="Stornoway (residence)">Stornoway</a>, the residence of the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Official Opposition</a>, but because of flooding in the basement, Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a> offered them <a href="/wiki/Harrington_Lake" title="Harrington Lake">Harrington Lake</a>, the prime minister's official country retreat in <a href="/wiki/Gatineau_Park" title="Gatineau Park">Gatineau Park</a>, with the expectation they would move into Stornoway at the start of July.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> However, the repairs were not complete, so Pierre Trudeau took a prolonged vacation with his sons to the <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a> summer home of his friend, Member of Parliament <a href="/wiki/Don_Johnston" title="Don Johnston">Don Johnston</a>, and later sent his sons to stay with their maternal grandparents in <a href="/wiki/North_Vancouver_(district_municipality)" title="North Vancouver (district municipality)">North Vancouver</a> for the rest of the summer while he slept at his friend's Ottawa apartment. Trudeau and his brothers returned to Ottawa for the start of the school year but lived only on the top floor of Stornoway while repairs continued on the bottom floor.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> His mother purchased and moved into a new home nearby at 95 Victoria Street in Ottawa's <a href="/wiki/New_Edinburgh" title="New Edinburgh">New Edinburgh</a> neighbourhood in September 1979.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Pierre Trudeau and his sons returned to the prime minister's official residence after <a href="/wiki/1980_Canadian_federal_election" title="1980 Canadian federal election">the February 1980 election</a> that returned him to the Prime Minister's Office.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_(1).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg/220px-Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg/330px-Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg/440px-Justint_Pierre_Elliott_Trudeau_%281%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="606" data-file-height="495" /></a><figcaption>10-year-old Justin touring the <a href="/wiki/Palais_des_Beaux-Arts_de_Lille" title="Palais des Beaux-Arts de Lille">Palais des Beaux-Arts de Lille</a> in France with <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">his father</a> in 1982</figcaption></figure>
<p>His father had intended Trudeau to begin his formal education at a French-language lycée, but Trudeau's mother convinced his father of the importance of sending their sons to a public school.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In the end, Trudeau was enrolled in 1976 in the <a href="/wiki/French_immersion" class="mw-redirect" title="French immersion">French immersion program</a> at <a href="/wiki/Rockcliffe_Park_Public_School" title="Rockcliffe Park Public School">Rockcliffe Park Public School</a>, the same school his mother had attended for two years while her father was a member of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He could have been dropped off by limousine, but his parents elected he take the <a href="/wiki/School_bus" title="School bus">school bus</a> albeit with a <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police" title="Royal Canadian Mounted Police">Royal Canadian Mounted Police</a> (RCMP) car following.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ottawa_Citizen1_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ottawa_Citizen1-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> This was followed by one year at the private <a href="/wiki/Lyc%C3%A9e_Claudel_d%27Ottawa" title="Lycée Claudel d&#39;Ottawa">Lycée Claudel d'Ottawa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Camp_Ahmek_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Camp_Ahmek-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After his father's retirement in June 1984, his mother remained at her New Edinburgh home while the rest of the family moved into his father's home at 1418 Pine Avenue, Montreal known as <a href="/wiki/Cormier_House" title="Cormier House">Cormier House</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> where the following autumn he began attending the private <a href="/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_Jean-de-Br%C3%A9beuf" title="Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf">Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf</a>, his father's alma mater. The school had begun as a Jesuit school but was non-denominational by the time Justin matriculated.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In 2008, Trudeau said that of all his early family outings he enjoyed camping with his father the most, because "that was where our father got to be just our father&#160;– a dad in the woods".<sup id="cite_ref-The_Son_Also_Rises_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Son_Also_Rises-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> During the summers his father would send him and his brothers to Camp Ahmek, on <a href="/wiki/Canoe_Lake_(Nipissing_District)" title="Canoe Lake (Nipissing District)">Canoe Lake</a> in <a href="/wiki/Algonquin_Provincial_Park" title="Algonquin Provincial Park">Algonquin Provincial Park</a>, where he would later work in his first paid job as a <a href="/wiki/Camp_counsellor" class="mw-redirect" title="Camp counsellor">camp counsellor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Camp_Ahmek_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Camp_Ahmek-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau and his brothers were given shares in two <a href="/wiki/Numbered_company" title="Numbered company">numbered companies</a> by their father: the first containing a portfolio of securities, from which they receive regular dividends, up to $20,000 per year; and the second which receives royalties from their father's autobiography and other sources, about $10,000 a year.<sup id="cite_ref-TrudeauEstate_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TrudeauEstate-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> As of August 2011, the first numbered company had assets of $1.2 million.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The Trudeau brothers were also given a country estate of about 50 hectares in the Laurentians with a home designed by the esteemed Canadian architect <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Erickson" title="Arthur Erickson">Arthur Erickson</a>, and the Cormier House in Montreal.<sup id="cite_ref-TrudeauArchitecture_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TrudeauArchitecture-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TrudeauEstate_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TrudeauEstate-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CountryEstate_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CountryEstate-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The country estate land was estimated to be worth $2.7 million in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-CountryEstate_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CountryEstate-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="University_and_early_career">University and early career</span></h3>
<p>Trudeau has a bachelor of arts degree in literature from <a href="/wiki/McGill_University" title="McGill University">McGill University</a> and a bachelor of education degree from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia" title="University of British Columbia">University of British Columbia</a>. In his first year at McGill, Trudeau became acquainted with his future <a href="/wiki/Principal_Secretary_(Canada)" title="Principal Secretary (Canada)">principal secretary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Butts" title="Gerald Butts">Gerald Butts</a>, through their mutual friend, Jonathan Ablett.<sup id="cite_ref-HuffPostBio_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HuffPostBio-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Butts invited Trudeau to join the McGill Debating Union.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> They bonded while driving back to Montreal after a debate tournament at <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-HuffPostBio_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HuffPostBio-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> After graduation, Trudeau stayed in Vancouver where he became a substitute teacher at local schools such as <a href="/wiki/Killarney_Secondary" class="mw-redirect" title="Killarney Secondary">Killarney Secondary</a> and worked permanently as a French and math teacher at the private <a href="/wiki/West_Point_Grey_Academy" title="West Point Grey Academy">West Point Grey Academy</a>. He became a roommate at the <a href="/wiki/List_of_heritage_buildings_in_Vancouver#Business" title="List of heritage buildings in Vancouver">Douglas Lodge</a><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> with fellow West Point Grey Academy faculty member and friend Christopher Ingvaldson.<sup id="cite_ref-HuffPostBio_68-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HuffPostBio-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> From 2002 to 2004, he studied engineering at the <a href="/wiki/%C3%89cole_Polytechnique_de_Montr%C3%A9al" class="mw-redirect" title="École Polytechnique de Montréal">École Polytechnique de Montréal</a>, affiliated with <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_de_Montr%C3%A9al" title="Université de Montréal">Université de Montréal</a>, but did not graduate.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> He started a master's degree in environmental geography at McGill but withdrew from the program to seek public office.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 2000, Justin Trudeau attended the Kokanee Summit in <a href="/wiki/Creston,_British_Columbia" title="Creston, British Columbia">Creston, British Columbia</a>, to raise funds in honour of his brother Michel Trudeau and other avalanche victims. After the event, an unsigned editorial in the <i>Creston Valley Advance</i> (a local newspaper) accused Trudeau of having groped an unnamed female reporter while at the music festival. The editorial stated Trudeau provided a "day-late" apology to the reporter, saying, "If I had known you were reporting for a national paper, I never would have been so forward".<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> In 2018, Trudeau was questioned about the groping incident but said he did not remember any negative incidents from that time. His apology and later statement about the event have been described as hypocritical, while responses to the story have been described as a <a href="/wiki/Witch-hunt" title="Witch-hunt">witch hunt</a> or non-story.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="float: right; clear: right; margin:0 0 1.5em 1.5em"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:115%">External video</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="text-align: left"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="video icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/16px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/24px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/32px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="128" data-file-height="128" /></span></span> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.cbc.ca/player/play/2565464845">"Trudeau delivers the eulogy at his father Pierre Trudeau's funeral".</a> – The National – CBC/Radio-Canada, October 3, 2000. (12:07 mins). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.cbc.ca/archives/entry/2000-justin-trudeau-delivers-eulogy-for-his-father-pierre">Archive (12:02 mins)</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In October 2000, Trudeau, then 28, emerged as a prominent figure after delivering a eulogy at <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Pierre_Trudeau" title="Death and state funeral of Pierre Trudeau">his father's state funeral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">Canadian Broadcasting Corporation</a> (CBC) received numerous calls to rebroadcast the speech after its initial transmission, and leading Quebec politician <a href="/wiki/Claude_Ryan" title="Claude Ryan">Claude Ryan</a> described it as "perhaps&#160;... the first manifestation of a dynasty".<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> A book issued by the CBC in 2003 included the speech in its list of significant Canadian events from the past fifty years.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2007, Trudeau starred in the two-part <a href="/wiki/CBC_Television" title="CBC Television">CBC Television</a> miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/The_Great_War_(2007_film)" title="The Great War (2007 film)">The Great War</a></i>, which gave an account of Canada's participation in the First World War. He portrayed his fifth cousin, twice removed,<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Major <a href="/wiki/Talbot_Mercer_Papineau" title="Talbot Mercer Papineau">Talbot Mercer Papineau</a>, who was killed on October 30, 1917, during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Passchendaele" title="Battle of Passchendaele">Battle of Passchendaele</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau is one of several children of former prime ministers who have become Canadian media personalities. The others are <a href="/wiki/Ben_Mulroney" title="Ben Mulroney">Ben Mulroney</a> (son of <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>), <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Clark_(broadcaster)" title="Catherine Clark (broadcaster)">Catherine Clark</a> (daughter of Joe Clark), and Trudeau's younger brother, Alexandre.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Ben Mulroney was a guest at Trudeau's wedding.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Advocacy">Advocacy</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg/220px-Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg/330px-Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg/440px-Justin_Trudeau_%C3%A0_la_Maison_des_Grands-Parents.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau (middle) in 2007 with "Maison des Grands-Parents" cofounders Lucille Girard and Jacqueline Desjardins, during the 15th anniversary of the non-profit seniors organization.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau and his family started the Kokanee Glacier Alpine Campaign for winter sports safety in 2000, two years after his brother Michel died in an avalanche during a ski trip.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> In 2002, Trudeau criticized the Government of British Columbia's decision to stop its funding for a public avalanche warning system.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 2002 to 2006, Trudeau chaired the <a href="/wiki/Katimavik" title="Katimavik">Katimavik</a> youth program, a project started by longtime family friend <a href="/wiki/Jacques_H%C3%A9bert_(Canadian_politician)" title="Jacques Hébert (Canadian politician)">Jacques Hébert</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2002–03, Trudeau was a panelist on <a href="/wiki/CBC_Radio" title="CBC Radio">CBC Radio</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Canada_Reads" title="Canada Reads">Canada Reads</a></i> series, where he championed <i><a href="/wiki/The_Colony_of_Unrequited_Dreams" title="The Colony of Unrequited Dreams">The Colony of Unrequited Dreams</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Johnston_(author)" class="mw-redirect" title="Wayne Johnston (author)">Wayne Johnston</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau and his brother Alexandre inaugurated the <a href="/wiki/Trudeau_Centre_for_Peace_and_Conflict_Studies" class="mw-redirect" title="Trudeau Centre for Peace and Conflict Studies">Trudeau Centre for Peace and Conflict Studies</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a> in April 2004; the centre later became a part of the <a href="/wiki/Munk_School_of_Global_Affairs" title="Munk School of Global Affairs">Munk School of Global Affairs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, he hosted the presentation ceremony for the <a href="/wiki/Giller_Prize" title="Giller Prize">Giller Prize</a> for literature.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2005, Trudeau fought against a proposed $100-million <a href="/wiki/Zinc" title="Zinc">zinc</a> mine that he argued would poison the <a href="/wiki/Nahanni_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Nahanni River">Nahanni River</a>, a <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_World_Heritage_Site" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations World Heritage Site">United Nations World Heritage Site</a> located in the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">Northwest Territories</a>. He was quoted as saying, "The river is an absolutely magnificent, magical place. I'm not saying mining is wrong&#160;... but that is not the place for it. It's just the wrong thing to be doing."<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 17, 2006, Trudeau was the master of ceremonies at a Toronto rally organized by <a href="/wiki/Rom%C3%A9o_Dallaire" title="Roméo Dallaire">Roméo Dallaire</a> that called for Canadian participation in resolving the <a href="/wiki/Darfur_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Darfur crisis">Darfur crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_beginnings">Political beginnings</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1,_rotated.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg/170px-Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg/255px-Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg/340px-Justin_Trudeau_supporting_Gerard_Kennedy_1%2C_rotated.jpg 2x" data-file-width="660" data-file-height="1086" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau at the <a href="/wiki/2006_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2006 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">2006 Liberal Party leadership convention</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau supported the Liberal Party from a young age, offering his support to party leader <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988 federal election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, he defended <a href="/wiki/Canadian_federalism" title="Canadian federalism">Canadian federalism</a> at a student event at the <a href="/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_Jean-de-Br%C3%A9beuf" title="Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf">Collège Jean-de-Brébeuf</a>, which he attended.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following his father's death, Trudeau became more involved with the Liberal Party throughout the 2000s. Along with Olympian <a href="/wiki/Charmaine_Crooks" title="Charmaine Crooks">Charmaine Crooks</a>, he co-hosted a tribute to outgoing prime minister <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a> at the party's <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_convention,_2003" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Canada leadership convention, 2003">2003 leadership convention</a>, and was appointed to chair a task force on youth renewal after the party's defeat in the <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006 federal election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2006, Trudeau criticized <a href="/wiki/Quebec_nationalism" title="Quebec nationalism">Quebec nationalism</a> by describing political nationalism generally as an "old idea from the 19th century", "based on a smallness of thought" and not relevant to modern Quebec. This comment was seen as a criticism of <a href="/wiki/Michael_Ignatieff" title="Michael Ignatieff">Michael Ignatieff</a>, then a candidate in the <a href="/wiki/2006_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2006 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">2006 Liberal Party leadership election</a>, who was promoting recognition of <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a> as a nation.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau later wrote a public letter on the subject, describing the idea of Quebec nationhood as "against everything my father ever believed".<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau announced his support for leadership candidate <a href="/wiki/Gerard_Kennedy" title="Gerard Kennedy">Gerard Kennedy</a> shortly before the 2006 convention and introduced Kennedy during the candidates' final speeches.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> When Kennedy dropped off after the second ballot, Trudeau joined him in supporting the ultimate winner, <a href="/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Dion" title="Stéphane Dion">Stéphane Dion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rumours circulated in early 2007 that Trudeau would run in an <a href="/wiki/By-elections_to_the_39th_Canadian_Parliament#Outremont" title="By-elections to the 39th Canadian Parliament">upcoming by-election</a> in the Montreal riding of <a href="/wiki/Outremont_(federal_electoral_district)" class="mw-redirect" title="Outremont (federal electoral district)">Outremont</a>. The Montreal newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/La_Presse_(Canadian_newspaper)" title="La Presse (Canadian newspaper)">La Presse</a></i> reported despite Trudeau's keenness, Liberal leader Stéphane Dion wanted Outremont for a star candidate who could help rebuild the Liberal Party. Instead, Trudeau announced that he would seek the Liberal nomination in the nearby riding of Papineau for the next general election.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The riding, which had been held for 26 years by <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Ouellet" title="André Ouellet">André Ouellet</a>, a senior minister under his father, had been in Liberal hands for 53 years before falling to the <a href="/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Bloc Québécois">Bloc Québécois</a> in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-Papineau_profile_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Papineau_profile-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On April 29, 2007, Trudeau won the Liberal party's nomination, picking up 690 votes to 350 for Deros and 220 for Giordano against <a href="/wiki/Mary_Deros" title="Mary Deros">Mary Deros</a>, a Montreal city councillor and <a href="/wiki/Basilio_Giordano" title="Basilio Giordano">Basilio Giordano</a>, the publisher of a local Italian-language newspaper.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition.2C_2008.E2.80.932015"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition,_2008–2015">Opposition, 2008–2015</span></h2>
<p>Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a> called an election for <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_election" title="2008 Canadian federal election">October 14, 2008</a>, by which time Trudeau had been campaigning for a year in Papineau. On election day, Trudeau narrowly defeated Bloc Québécois incumbent <a href="/wiki/Vivian_Barbot" title="Vivian Barbot">Vivian Barbot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Following his election win, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Greenspon" title="Edward Greenspon">Edward Greenspon</a>, editor-in-chief of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Globe_and_Mail" title="The Globe and Mail">The Globe and Mail</a></i>, noted that Trudeau would "be viewed as few other rookie MPs are—as a potential future Prime Minister—and scrutinized through that lens".<sup id="cite_ref-The_Son_Also_Rises_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Son_Also_Rises-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg/170px-Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg/255px-Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg/340px-Trudeau_headshot_2008.jpg 2x" data-file-width="719" data-file-height="990" /></a><figcaption>2008 Trudeau promotional portrait by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Marc_Carisse" title="Jean-Marc Carisse">Jean-Marc Carisse</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party</a> won a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> in the 2008 election, and Trudeau entered parliament as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official Opposition</a>. Trudeau's first legislative act was a motion that called for the creation of a "national voluntary service policy for young people".<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> He later co-chaired the Liberal Party's April 2009 national convention in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, and in October of the same year he was appointed as the party's critic for multiculturalism and youth.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2010, he was reassigned as critic for youth, citizenship, and immigration.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> During that time, he criticized the government's legislation targeting human smuggling, which he argued would penalize the victims of smuggling.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau sparked controversy when it was revealed that he earned $1.3 million in public speaking fees from charities and school boards across Canada, $277,000 of which Trudeau received after becoming an MP.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He encouraged an increase of Canada's relief efforts after the <a href="/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake" title="2010 Haiti earthquake">2010 Haiti earthquake</a>, and sought more accessible immigration procedures for Haitians moving to Canada in the time of crisis. His own riding includes a significant Haitian community.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau was re-elected in Papineau in the <a href="/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election" title="2011 Canadian federal election">2011 federal election</a>, as the Liberal Party fell to third-party standing in the House of Commons with only thirty-four seats. Ignatieff resigned as party leader immediately after the election, and rumours again circulated that Trudeau could run to become his successor. On this occasion, Trudeau said, "I don't feel I should be closing off any options&#160;... because of the history packaged into my name, a lot of people are turning to me in a way that&#160;... to be blunt, concerns me."<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Weeks after the election, Toronto MP <a href="/wiki/Bob_Rae" title="Bob Rae">Bob Rae</a> was selected as the interim leader until the party's leadership convention, which was later decided to be held in April 2013. Rae appointed Trudeau as the party's critic for post-secondary education, youth and amateur sport.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> After his re-election, he travelled the country hosting fundraisers for charities and the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau wanted to take part in a charity boxing match on behalf of the cancer research fundraising event Fight for the Cure, but was having difficulty finding a Conservative opponent until Conservative senator <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Brazeau" title="Patrick Brazeau">Patrick Brazeau</a> agreed when asked on Trudeau's behalf by their mutual hairdresser Stefania Capovilla.<sup id="cite_ref-boxing_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-boxing-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The fight on March 31, 2012, in Ottawa at the Hampton Inn was broadcast live on <a href="/wiki/Sun_News_Network" title="Sun News Network">Sun News</a> with commentary by <a href="/wiki/Ezra_Levant" title="Ezra Levant">Ezra Levant</a> and <a href="/wiki/Brian_Lilley" title="Brian Lilley">Brian Lilley</a> and Trudeau won in the third round, the result considered an upset.<sup id="cite_ref-boxing_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-boxing-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CTV_boxing_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CTV_boxing-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Liberal_Party">Leader of the Liberal Party</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Earlier_speculation">Earlier speculation</span></h4>
<p>After Dion's resignation as Liberal leader in 2008, Trudeau's name was mentioned as a potential candidate with polls showing him as a favourite among Canadians for the position.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Trudeau did not enter the race and <a href="/wiki/Michael_Ignatieff" title="Michael Ignatieff">Michael Ignatieff</a> was named leader in December 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> After the party's poor showing in the 2011 election, Ignatieff resigned from the leadership and Trudeau was again seen as a potential candidate to lead the party.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the election, Trudeau said he was undecided about seeking the leadership;<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> months later on October 12 at <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier_University" title="Wilfrid Laurier University">Wilfrid Laurier University</a>, he announced he would not seek the post because he had a young family.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> When interim leader <a href="/wiki/Bob_Rae" title="Bob Rae">Bob Rae</a>, who was also seen as a frontrunner, announced he would not be entering the race in June 2012, Trudeau was hit with a "tsunami" of calls from supporters to reconsider his earlier decision to not seek the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Opinion polling conducted by several pollsters showed that if Trudeau were to become leader the Liberal Party would surge in support, from a distant third place to either being competitive with the Conservative Party or leading them.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> In July 2012, Trudeau stated that he would reconsider his earlier decision to not seek the leadership and would announce his final decision at the end of the summer.<sup id="cite_ref-Considering_leadership_run_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Considering_leadership_run-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Not_committed_to_leadership_run_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Not_committed_to_leadership_run-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2013_leadership_election">2013 leadership election</span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2013_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2013 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">2013 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election</a></div>
<p>On September 26, 2012, multiple media outlets started reporting that Trudeau would launch his leadership bid the following week.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> While Trudeau was seen as a frontrunner for the leadership of the Liberal Party, he was criticized for his perceived lack of substance.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> During his time as a member of Parliament, he spoke little on policy matters and it was not known where he stood on many issues such as the economy and foreign affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Some strategists and pundits believed the leadership would be the time for Trudeau to be tested on these issues; however, there was also fear within the party that his celebrity status and large lead might deter other strong candidates from entering the leadership race.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On October 2, 2012, Trudeau held a rally in Montreal to launch his bid for the leadership of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Wall_Street_Journal_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Wall_Street_Journal-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The core people on his campaign team were considered longtime friends, and all in their 30s and 40s. His senior advisor was Gerald Butts, the former president of <a href="/wiki/WWF-Canada" title="WWF-Canada">WWF-Canada</a> who had previously been principal secretary to former Ontario premier <a href="/wiki/Dalton_McGuinty" title="Dalton McGuinty">Dalton McGuinty</a>. Other senior aides included campaign manager <a href="/wiki/Katie_Telford" title="Katie Telford">Katie Telford</a>, and policy advisors Mike McNeir and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Asselin" title="Robert Asselin">Robert Asselin</a>, who had all worked for recent Liberal Party leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> His brother Alexandre also took a break from his documentary work to be a senior advisor on Trudeau's campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the leadership campaign three by-elections were held on <a href="/wiki/By-elections_to_the_41st_Canadian_Parliament#November_26,_2012_by-elections" title="By-elections to the 41st Canadian Parliament">November 26, 2012</a>. The riding <a href="/wiki/Calgary_Centre" title="Calgary Centre">Calgary Centre</a> was expected to be a three-way race between the Conservatives, Liberals and Green Party. A week before by-election day <a href="/wiki/Sun_Media" title="Sun Media">Sun Media</a> reported on comments Trudeau had made in a 2010 interview with <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A9l%C3%A9-Qu%C3%A9bec" title="Télé-Québec">Télé-Québec</a>, in which he said, "Canada isn't doing well right now because it's Albertans who control our community and socio-democratic agenda." Trudeau's campaign advisor said that the comments were being brought up now because of the close race in Calgary Centre.<sup id="cite_ref-alta_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-alta-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The following day, Trudeau apologized, saying he was wrong to use "Alberta" as "<a href="/wiki/Shorthand" title="Shorthand">shorthand</a>" in referring to Stephen Harper's government.<sup id="cite_ref-alta2_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-alta2-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives held onto Calgary Centre in the by-election by less than 1,200 votes. Liberal candidate <a href="/wiki/Harvey_Locke" title="Harvey Locke">Harvey Locke</a> said he lost the by-election on his own and that comments made by Trudeau did not influence the outcome.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fellow leadership candidate <a href="/wiki/Marc_Garneau" title="Marc Garneau">Marc Garneau</a>, seen as Trudeau's main challenger in the race, criticized Trudeau for not releasing enough substantial policy positions. Garneau called on him to release more detailed policies before members and supporters begin to vote.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Garneau later challenged Trudeau to a one-on-one debate, and said that if Trudeau could not defend his ideas in a debate against him, he wouldn't be able to do so against Prime Minister Harper.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau clashed in debates with challenger <a href="/wiki/Joyce_Murray" title="Joyce Murray">Joyce Murray</a>, who was the only Liberal leadership candidate to speak out strongly in favour of electing the House of Commons with a system of <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a>. She challenged Trudeau over his support for a <a href="/wiki/Preferential_ballot" class="mw-redirect" title="Preferential ballot">preferential ballot</a> voting system.<sup id="cite_ref-Murray_and_Trudeau_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Murray_and_Trudeau-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On March 13, 2013, Garneau dropped out of the leadership race, saying that polling conducted by his campaign showed he would be unable to defeat Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Joyce Murray, the last challenger, receiving significant press time, more Liberal politicians and public figures declared themselves for Trudeau. Trudeau was declared the winner of the leadership election on April 14, 2013, garnering 80.1 per cent of 30,800 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Joyce Murray finished in second place with 10.2 per cent points, ahead of Martha Hall Findlay's 5.7 per cent.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau had lost only five ridings, all to Murray and all in BC.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span id="Leadership.2C_2013.E2.80.932015"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership,_2013–2015">Leadership, 2013–2015</span></h5>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg/170px-Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg/255px-Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/ef/Justin_Trudeau_2014-1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="340" data-file-height="471" /></a><figcaption>Justin Trudeau delivering a speech on a doorstep in <a href="/wiki/Little_Italy,_Toronto" title="Little Italy, Toronto">Toronto's Little Italy</a>, 2014<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the days following his victory in the leadership race, snapshot polls recorded a surge in support for the Liberal party.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2013, Trudeau chose to give up his seat at the <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Nelson_Mandela" title="Death and state funeral of Nelson Mandela">funeral of Nelson Mandela</a>, in deference to <a href="/wiki/Irwin_Cotler" title="Irwin Cotler">Irwin Cotler</a> as representative of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party of Canada</a>, because of Cotler's work for and with <a href="/wiki/Nelson_Mandela" title="Nelson Mandela">Nelson Mandela</a> in fighting <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the leadership campaign Trudeau pledged to park all his assets, exclusive of real estate holdings, into a <a href="/wiki/Blind_trust" title="Blind trust">blind trust</a> which is atypical for opposition MPs, including leaders. According to documents obtained by the <i><a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Citizen" title="Ottawa Citizen">Ottawa Citizen</a></i>, he fulfilled the pledge in July 2013 when the blind trust was set up by <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Montreal" title="Bank of Montreal">BMO Private Banking</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On January 27, 2014, Trudeau and MP <a href="/wiki/Carolyn_Bennett" title="Carolyn Bennett">Carolyn Bennett</a> escorted <a href="/wiki/Chrystia_Freeland" title="Chrystia Freeland">Chrystia Freeland</a> into the House of Commons, as is traditional for by-election victors.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau launched an internet video the week before the 2014 Liberal party convention titled "An economy that benefits us all" in which he narrates his economic platform. He said that Canada's debt to GDP ratios have come down in recent years and now it's time for Ottawa to "step up".<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2015_federal_election">2015 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg/220px-Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg/330px-Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg/440px-Justin_Trudeau_at_the_Vancouver_LGBTQ_Pride_2015.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau at the <a href="/wiki/Vancouver_Pride_Festival" class="mw-redirect" title="Vancouver Pride Festival">Vancouver Pride Festival</a>, shortly after launching his election campaign</figcaption></figure>
<p>On October 19, 2015, after the longest official campaign in over a century, Trudeau led the Liberals to a decisive victory in the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">federal election</a>. The Liberals won 184 of the 338 seats, with 39.5% of the popular vote, for a strong majority government;<sup id="cite_ref-cbc.ca_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc.ca-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> a gain of 150 seats compared to the 2011 federal election.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc.ca_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc.ca-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This was the second-best performance in the party's history. The Liberals won mostly on the strength of a solid performance in the eastern half of the country. In addition to taking all of Atlantic Canada and Toronto,<sup id="cite_ref-cbc.ca_174-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc.ca-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> they won 40 seats in Quebec—the most that the Liberals had won in that province since Trudeau's father led them to a near-sweep of the province in 1980, and also the first time since then that the Liberals won a majority of Quebec's seats in an election. The 150-seat gain was the biggest numerical increase for a single party since Confederation and marked the first time that a party had rebounded from third place in the Commons to a majority government.
</p><p>In addition to the appeal of his party's platform, Trudeau's success has been credited to his performance both on the campaign trail and televised <a href="/wiki/Canadian_leaders%27_debates" title="Canadian leaders&#39; debates">leaders' debates</a> exceeding the lowered expectations created by Conservative advertisements and conservative media outlets.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:RealChange.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/RealChange.png/220px-RealChange.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="43" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/RealChange.png/330px-RealChange.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/RealChange.png/440px-RealChange.png 2x" data-file-width="547" data-file-height="106" /></a><figcaption>The Trudeau Liberals slogan during the 2015 campaign was "Real Change"</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau declared victory shortly after <a href="/wiki/CBC_News" title="CBC News">CBC News</a> projected that he had won a majority government. He began his speech with a reference to former Liberal prime minister <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>'s "sunny ways" (French: <i lang="fr">voies ensoleillées</i>) approach to bringing Canadians together despite their differences. According to Trudeau, Laurier "knew that politics can be a positive force, and that's the message Canadians have sent today".<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Harper announced his resignation as the leader of the Conservative Party that night.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_of_Canada_.282015.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_of_Canada_(2015–present)">Prime Minister of Canada (2015–present)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Justin_Trudeau" title="Premiership of Justin Trudeau">Premiership of Justin Trudeau</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg/220px-Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg/330px-Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg/440px-Swearing-In_Day_Trudeau_Cabinet.jpg 2x" data-file-width="810" data-file-height="540" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/29th_Canadian_Ministry" title="29th Canadian Ministry">29th Canadian Ministry</a> after being sworn into office, November 4, 2015</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau and the rest of the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">Cabinet</a> were sworn in by <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> <a href="/wiki/David_Johnston" title="David Johnston">David Johnston</a> on November 4, 2015. He said that his first legislative priority was to lower taxes for middle-income Canadians and raise taxes for the top one per cent of income earners after parliament was reconvened on December 3, 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau also issued a statement promising to rebuild relations with <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Canada" title="Indigenous peoples in Canada">Indigenous peoples in Canada</a> and run an open, ethical and transparent government.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> On November 5, 2015, during the first Liberal caucus meeting since forming a majority government, the party announced that it would reinstate the mandatory long-form census that had been scrapped in 2010, effective with the 2016 census.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Prime Minister, Trudeau launched three major independent investigations: the <a href="/wiki/National_Inquiry_into_Missing_and_Murdered_Indigenous_Women" title="National Inquiry into Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women">National Inquiry into Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women</a> (MMIWG), the Joint Federal/Provincial Commission into the <a href="/wiki/2020_Nova_Scotia_attacks" title="2020 Nova Scotia attacks">2020 Nova Scotia attacks</a> (in partnership with the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Government of Nova Scotia">Government of Nova Scotia</a><sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>), and the Public Inquiry into Foreign Interference in Federal Electoral Processes and Democratic Institutions. The latter was called in response to <a href="/wiki/Allegations_of_Chinese_government_interference_in_the_2019_and_2021_Canadian_federal_elections" class="mw-redirect" title="Allegations of Chinese government interference in the 2019 and 2021 Canadian federal elections">allegations of Chinese government interference in the 2019 and 2021 Canadian federal elections</a>, but also deals with interference from other states deemed hostile to Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The MMIWG investigation found that Canada's response to this issue amounts to <a href="/wiki/Genocide_of_Indigenous_peoples" title="Genocide of Indigenous peoples">genocide</a>, a finding Justin Trudeau Trudeau said he accepted.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as Prime Minister, Justin Trudeau has been the target of multiple death threats and assassination plots.<sup id="cite_ref-:7_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethics">Ethics</span></h3><p>
