Trudeau was criticized by opposition members in November 2016 for his fundraising tactics which they saw as "cash for access" schemes.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau attended fundraisers where attendees paid an upward of $1500 for access to him and other cabinet members.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:2_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> In some instances, the events were attended by foreign businessmen who needed government approval for their businesses.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau defended his fundraising tactics, saying that they were not in breach of any ethics rules. He also stated that he was lobbied at the fundraisers but not influenced.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_192-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> In 2017, Trudeau introduced legislation that would eliminate such exclusive events by requiring increased transparency for political fundraisers.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="244" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-State_Visit_of_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_17_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="461" data-file-height="663" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau walks with US President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> along the Colonnade to the <a href="/wiki/Oval_Office" title="Oval Office">Oval Office</a>, March 10, 2016</figcaption></figure>
<p>In January 2017, the <a href="/wiki/Ethics_Commissioner_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethics Commissioner (Canada)">ethics commissioner</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mary_Dawson_(civil_servant)" title="Mary Dawson (civil servant)">Mary Dawson</a>, began an investigation into Trudeau for a vacation he and his family took to <a href="/wiki/Aga_Khan_IV" title="Aga Khan IV">Aga Khan IV</a>'s private island in the <a href="/wiki/Bahamas" class="mw-redirect" title="Bahamas">Bahamas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-veconomist_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-veconomist-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> The ethics commissioner's report, released in December 2017, found that Trudeau had violated four provisions of the <i>Conflict of Interest Act</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> He became the first sitting prime minister to break federal conflict of interest rules.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, it was reported that the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police" title="Royal Canadian Mounted Police">Royal Canadian Mounted Police</a> had considered bringing criminal charges against Trudeau over the affair.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2018, Trudeau was criticized when his government invited <a href="/wiki/Khalistan_movement" title="Khalistan movement">Khalistani nationalist</a> Jaspal Atwal to the Canadian High Commission's dinner party in Delhi. Atwal had previously been convicted for the shooting and attempted murder of Indian Cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Malkiat_Singh_Sidhu" title="Malkiat Singh Sidhu">Malkiat Singh Sidhu</a> in 1986, as well as the assault on former BC premier <a href="/wiki/Ujjal_Dosanjh" title="Ujjal Dosanjh">Ujjal Dosanjh</a> in 1985. Following the dinner, the PMO rescinded the invitation, and apologized for the incident.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="SNC-Lavalin_affair">SNC-Lavalin affair</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/SNC-Lavalin_affair" title="SNC-Lavalin affair">SNC-Lavalin affair</a></div>
<p>On February 8, 2019, <i>The Globe and Mail</i> reported that sources close to the government said that the Prime Minister's Office had allegedly attempted to influence <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada" title="Minister of Justice and Attorney General of Canada">Attorney General</a> <a href="/wiki/Jody_Wilson-Raybould" title="Jody Wilson-Raybould">Jody Wilson-Raybould</a> concerning an ongoing prosecution of <a href="/wiki/SNC-Lavalin" class="mw-redirect" title="SNC-Lavalin">SNC-Lavalin</a>. When asked about the allegations, Trudeau said that the story in the <i>Globe</i> was false and that he had never "directed" Wilson-Raybould concerning the case.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Wilson-Raybould did not comment on the matter, citing <a href="/wiki/Legal_professional_privilege" title="Legal professional privilege">solicitor-client privilege</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Soon after, Trudeau voluntarily waived privilege and cabinet confidences, permitting her to speak.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> On February 11, the ethics commissioner announced the opening of an investigation into the allegations. Trudeau said he "welcomed the investigation".<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Canadian_House_of_Commons_Standing_Committee_on_Justice_and_Human_Rights" title="Canadian House of Commons Standing Committee on Justice and Human Rights">Justice Committee</a> of the House of Commons has conducted a series of hearings on the alleged interference.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-211">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> The investigation heard from several witnesses, including Jody Wilson-Raybould, who submitted as evidence a telephone call she secretly recorded between herself and <a href="/wiki/Clerk_of_the_Privy_Council_(Canada)" title="Clerk of the Privy Council (Canada)">Privy Council Clerk</a> <a href="/wiki/Michael_Wernick" title="Michael Wernick">Michael Wernick</a>, which was subsequently released to the public.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> On the recording, Wernick is heard asking to understand why the "DPA route" is not being used, stating that people were "talking past each other", and suggesting Trudeau obtain independent legal advice from former Supreme Court chief justice <a href="/wiki/Beverley_McLachlin" title="Beverley McLachlin">Beverly McLachlin</a>. Wilson-Raybould is heard suggesting that Trudeau would be "breaching a constitutional principle of prosecutorial independence". On March 19, 2019, the Liberal committee members voted as a bloc to shut down the Justice Committee's investigation.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau was the subject of an investigation by the <a href="/wiki/Ethics_Commissioner_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethics Commissioner (Canada)">ethics commissioner</a>, pursuant to the <i>Conflict of Interest Act</i>, in regards to <a href="/wiki/Criminal_Code_(Canada)" title="Criminal Code (Canada)">criminal charges</a> against <a href="/wiki/SNC-Lavalin" class="mw-redirect" title="SNC-Lavalin">SNC-Lavalin</a> in the <a href="/wiki/SNC-Lavalin_affair" title="SNC-Lavalin affair">SNC-Lavalin affair</a>. The commission's final report, issued August 14, 2019, concluded "Mr. Trudeau contravened section 9 of the Act".<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_federal_election">2019 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2019_Canadian_federal_election" title="2019 Canadian federal election">2019 Canadian federal election</a></div><p>On September 11, 2019, Trudeau visited Governor General <a href="/wiki/Julie_Payette" title="Julie Payette">Julie Payette</a>, to request the dissolution of Parliament, and formally triggering an election.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Prior to the formal start of the campaign, Trudeau announced his intention to only participate in the three <a href="/wiki/Canadian_leaders%27_debates#2019_debates" title="Canadian leaders&#39; debates">leaders' debates</a>, two organized by the <a href="/wiki/Leaders%27_Debates_Commission" title="Leaders&#39; Debates Commission">Leaders' Debates Commission</a>, and one organized by <a href="/wiki/TVA_(Canadian_TV_network)" title="TVA (Canadian TV network)">TVA</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-TVAdeb_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TVAdeb-220">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> Other leader's debates were either cancelled or took place with an empty podium left on stage for Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-Snub_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Snub-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau,_Juno_Beach,_6_juin_2019_(48071928046).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg/170px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg/255px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg/340px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Juno_Beach%2C_6_juin_2019_%2848071928046%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1521" data-file-height="1862" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau speaking at <a href="/wiki/Juno_Beach" title="Juno Beach">Juno Beach</a> during the 75th anniversary of <a href="/wiki/D-Day" class="mw-redirect" title="D-Day">D-Day</a>, June 6, 2019</figcaption></figure><p>In September 2019, controversial pictures and video were published showing Trudeau in <a href="/wiki/Racial_brownface" class="mw-redirect" title="Racial brownface">brownface</a> and <a href="/wiki/Blackface" title="Blackface">blackface</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> On September 18, 2019, <a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)"><i>Time</i></a> magazine published a photograph of Trudeau wearing brownface makeup in the spring of 2001, at an <i><a href="/wiki/Arabian_Nights" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabian Nights">Arabian Nights</a></i>-themed gala, while Trudeau was a teacher at <a href="/wiki/West_Point_Grey_Academy" title="West Point Grey Academy">West Point Grey Academy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau publicly apologized, agreeing the photo was racist and saying: "I shouldn't have done that. I should have known better and I didn't. I'm really sorry."<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> He further went on to say "It was something that I didn't think was racist at the time, but now I recognize it was something racist to do".<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau also admitted to wearing blackface makeup in high school while singing "<a href="/wiki/Day-O_(The_Banana_Boat_Song)" title="Day-O (The Banana Boat Song)">Day-O</a>" at a talent show that was subsequently published by <a href="/wiki/Global_News" title="Global News">Global News</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> A third instance, a video, of Trudeau in racist dress was also published.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> After this video was published, Trudeau admitted he could not remember how often he had worn blackface makeup.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> In the days following the scandal, <a href="/wiki/Opinion_poll" title="Opinion poll">pollsters</a> pointed out that many Canadians either were not bothered by the scandal or had accepted Trudeau's apology.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, some minority community groups, racialized commentators and some of Trudeau's opponents came to his defence.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Others were more critical, including members of his own party.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_election_results">2019 election results</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_(Simple).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg/260px-Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="260" height="203" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg/390px-Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg/520px-Canada_Election_2019_Results_Map_%28Simple%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1046" data-file-height="818" /></a><figcaption>Canadian federal election 2019 showing the ridings by winning party colour</figcaption></figure>
<p>While Trudeau's Liberal Party lost 20 seats in the House of Commons (lowering its total from 177 to 157) from the time of dissolution, they still won the most seats of any party—enough seats to allow Trudeau to form a minority government.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> For the first time since 1979, the party that garnered the largest share of the national popular vote did not win the most seats; the Liberals under Trudeau had 33.1 per cent of the popular vote, while the Conservatives under <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Scheer" title="Andrew Scheer">Andrew Scheer</a> had 34.4 per cent.<sup id="cite_ref-toronto_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-toronto-241">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> It was also the first time a government took power with less than 35 per cent of the national popular vote since the Conservatives of <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>, in 1867, who had 34.8 per cent of the votes.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> In Alberta and Saskatchewan, the Liberal party won 14 per cent and 10 per cent of the popular vote, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> In Ontario, Liberals won all 25 Toronto seats and 24 of 29 seats in the surrounding suburbs of the <a href="/wiki/Greater_Toronto_Area" title="Greater Toronto Area">Greater Toronto Area</a>—reportedly due in part to the unpopularity of the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Ontario" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Ontario">Progressive Conservative party</a> government of Premier <a href="/wiki/Doug_Ford" title="Doug Ford">Doug Ford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-toronto_241-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-toronto-241">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="COVID-19_pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">COVID-19 pandemic in Canada</a></div>
<p>Justin Trudeau was Prime Minister during the worldwide <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a>. His government's response to the pandemic included funds for provinces and territories to adapt to the new situation, funds for coronavirus research, travel restrictions, screening of international flights, self-isolation orders under the <a href="/wiki/Quarantine_Act,_2005" title="Quarantine Act, 2005"><i>Quarantine Act</i></a>, an industrial strategy, and a public health awareness campaign. Initially, Canada faced a shortage of <a href="/wiki/Personal_protective_equipment" title="Personal protective equipment">personal protective equipment</a>, as the Trudeau government had cut PPE stockpile funding in the previous years.<sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To deal with the <a href="/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="Economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">economic impact of the pandemic</a> in 2020, Trudeau waived student loan payments, increased the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Child_Benefit" title="Canada Child Benefit">Canada Child Benefit</a>, doubled the annual <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_services_tax_(Canada)" title="Goods and services tax (Canada)">Goods and Services Tax</a> payment, and introduced the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Emergency_Response_Benefit" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Emergency Response Benefit">Canada Emergency Response Benefit</a> (CERB) as part of a first package in March. In April 2020, Trudeau introduced the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Emergency_Wage_Subsidy" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Emergency Wage Subsidy">Canada Emergency Wage Subsidy</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Emergency_Business_Account" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Emergency Business Account">Canada Emergency Business Account</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Emergency_Student_Benefit" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Emergency Student Benefit">Canada Emergency Student Benefit</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau also deployed the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_armed_forces" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian armed forces">Canadian Forces</a> in long-term care homes in Quebec and Ontario as part of <a href="/wiki/Operation_LASER" class="mw-redirect" title="Operation LASER">Operation LASER</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-mbafd_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mbafd-249">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout the pandemic, the federal government was also responsible for the procurement of <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_vaccine" title="COVID-19 vaccine">COVID-19 vaccines</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-250">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> On May 12, 2020, the Trudeau government announced it had reached an exclusive deal with <a href="/wiki/CanSino_Biologics" title="CanSino Biologics">CanSino Biologics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> However, due to deteriorating <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93China_relations" title="Canada–China relations">Canadian-Chinese relations</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Convidecia" title="Convidecia">Cansino</a> deal fell through.<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> On August 5, 2020, the Trudeau government created a plan to secure doses of the <a href="/wiki/Pfizer%E2%80%93BioNTech_COVID-19_vaccine" title="Pfizer–BioNTech COVID-19 vaccine">Pfizer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Moderna_COVID-19_vaccine" title="Moderna COVID-19 vaccine">Moderna vaccines</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> Starting in December 2020, Justin Trudeau oversaw the implementation of <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 vaccination in Canada">Canada's mass-vaccination program</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-aljazeera_20201127_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aljazeera_20201127-254">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_250-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-250">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The spread of COVID-19 in Canada continued beyond the initial outbreak, with a strong second wave in the fall of 2020 and an even more serious third wave in the spring of 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the crisis, Trudeau periodically extended the scope and duration of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_aid_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="Federal aid during the COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">federal aid programs</a>. The <a href="/wiki/2021_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2021 Canadian federal budget">2021 Canadian federal budget</a> planned to phase them out by the end of September 2021, and projected a $354.2-billion deficit in the 2020-21 fiscal year.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> While CERB was indeed phased out on September 26, the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Recovery_Benefit" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Recovery Benefit">Canada Recovery Benefit</a> (CBR) continued to provide support until October 23.<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Canada_Worker_Lockdown_Benefit" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Worker Lockdown Benefit">Canada Worker Lockdown Benefit</a> was introduced that month to replace the CBR, and expanded during the spread of the <a href="/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant" title="SARS-CoV-2 Omicron variant">Omicron variant</a> in December 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="WE_Charity_ethics_investigation">WE Charity ethics investigation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/WE_Charity_scandal" title="WE Charity scandal">WE Charity scandal</a></div>
<p>Following complaints by opposition parties that the Trudeau family had ties to <a href="/wiki/WE_Charity" title="WE Charity">WE Charity</a>, the ethics commissioner on July 3, 2020, announced an investigation into Trudeau's and the government's decision to have the charity administer a summer, student-grant program which could assist students financially during the COVID-19 pandemic. Trudeau responded by saying WE was the charity that had the capability to administer such a program. WE and the federal government decided to "part ways" leaving administration of the grant program to the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Tasker1_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tasker1-262">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>WE Charity was criticized for its close ties to the Trudeau family; the investigation came after revelations that Trudeau's mother, brother, and wife were paid nearly $300,000 in total to speak at WE Charity events.<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup>
On July 16, 2020, the ethics commissioner also announced the investigation was being expanded to include Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Bill_Morneau" title="Bill Morneau">Bill Morneau</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau was ultimately cleared of any wrongdoing by the ethics commissioner though Morneau was found to have broken the conflict of interest law.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2021_federal_election">2021 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2021_Canadian_federal_election" title="2021 Canadian federal election">2021 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>On August 15, 2021, Trudeau advised Governor General <a href="/wiki/Mary_Simon" title="Mary Simon">Mary Simon</a> to dissolve parliament, scheduling an election for September 20.<sup id="cite_ref-ctv081521_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ctv081521-268">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> The election was called on the same day as the <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_(2021)" title="Fall of Kabul (2021)">Fall of Kabul</a>. In the first two weeks of the campaign, Trudeau received criticism for not acting fast enough in the face of the <a href="/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive" title="2021 Taliban offensive">2021 Taliban offensive</a> to evacuate Canadian citizens and Afghans who supported Canada’s military and diplomatic efforts during the <a href="/wiki/Canada_in_the_War_in_Afghanistan" title="Canada in the War in Afghanistan">War in Afghanistan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 2021 federal election, Trudeau secured a third mandate and his second minority government after winning 160 seats. However, the Liberals came in second in the national popular vote, behind the Conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> They received 32.6 percent of the popular vote, the lowest percentage of the national popular vote for a governing party in Canadian history.<sup id="cite_ref-Hopper2_271-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hopper2-271">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2022_convoy_protest">2022 convoy protest</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Canada_convoy_protest" title="Canada convoy protest">Canada convoy protest</a></div>
<p>The Canada convoy protest, called the Freedom Convoy, was a <a href="/wiki/Protests_over_responses_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic" class="mw-redirect" title="Protests over responses to the COVID-19 pandemic">protest</a> in Canada against <a href="/wiki/Vaccine_passports_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic#Canada" title="Vaccine passports during the COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 vaccine requirements for truckers to re-enter the country by land</a> introduced by the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Canada" title="Government of Canada">Government of Canada</a> on January 15, 2022. Originally composed of several routes traversing all of the Canadian <a href="/wiki/Provinces_and_territories_of_Canada" title="Provinces and territories of Canada">provinces</a>, the truck convoys converged on <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>.
</p><p>On January 29, the first day of protest at <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a>, Prime Minister Trudeau moved to an undisclosed location.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_Tasker_20220129_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC_Tasker_20220129-272">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> According to <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i>, the demonstration developed to express a number of "antigovernment grievances", particularly against Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-theguardian_Cecco_20220128_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theguardian_Cecco_20220128-274">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> On January 31, Trudeau called the protests an "insult to truth".<sup id="cite_ref-Insult_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Insult-275">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> On February 3, he said that a military response was "not in the cards right now".<sup id="cite_ref-276" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-276">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> On February 11, <i><a href="/wiki/Reuters" title="Reuters">Reuters</a></i> reported that Trudeau promised the US "quick action" regarding protesters who have forcefully blocked the Ambassador Bridge on the US-Canada border, the continent's busiest land border crossing."<sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau subsequently indicated that there would be "robust police intervention" and called for all protesters to "go home."<sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Trudeau invoked the <i><a href="/wiki/Emergencies_Act" title="Emergencies Act">Emergencies Act</a></i> on February 14, 2022, for the first time since it was enacted in 1988, as a result of the public order emergency caused by the demonstrations in Ottawa.<sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup> On February 23, 2022, Trudeau announced that the federal government would revoke the emergency declaration. Later that day, the governor general signed a proclamation revoking it.<sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>  A year later, on February 17, 2023, a judicial inquiry into the use of the <i>Emergencies Act</i> concluded that the Trudeau government met the legal threshold required to invoke the act.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Policies">Policies</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policy">Domestic policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_Justin_Trudeau_government" title="Domestic policy of the Justin Trudeau government">Domestic policy of the Justin Trudeau government</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_(34569966804).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg/220px-Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg/330px-Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg/440px-Women_Deliver_2019_Conference_Location_Announcement_%2834569966804%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1996" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau with <a href="/wiki/Maryam_Monsef" title="Maryam Monsef">Maryam Monsef</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marie-Claude_Bibeau" title="Marie-Claude Bibeau">Marie-Claude Bibeau</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Women_Deliver" title="Women Deliver">2019 Women Deliver event</a> in Vancouver.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Trudeau government's <a href="/wiki/Economic_policy" title="Economic policy">economic policy</a> initially relied on increased tax revenues to pay for increased government spending. While the government did not balance the budget in its first term, it reduced Canada's <a href="/wiki/Debt-to-GDP_ratio" title="Debt-to-GDP ratio">debt-to-GDP ratio</a> every year until 2020, when the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">COVID-19 pandemic</a> hit.<sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-284" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-284">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau's self-described progressive and feminist <a href="/wiki/Social_policy" title="Social policy">social policy</a> has included strong advocacy for abortion rights.<sup id="cite_ref-HeatherSaul_285-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HeatherSaul-285">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> His government introduced the bill that made <a href="/wiki/Conversion_therapy" title="Conversion therapy">conversion therapies</a> illegal in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his first term, Canada set targets to welcome an increased number of immigrants and refugees.<sup id="cite_ref-Syrians_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Syrians-287">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Immigrants2018_288-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Immigrants2018-288">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> Canada introduced the right to <a href="/wiki/Voluntary_euthanasia" title="Voluntary euthanasia">medically-assisted dying</a> in 2016<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_289-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC-289">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Cannabis_in_Canada" title="Cannabis in Canada">legalized cannabis for recreational use</a> in 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-WEED_290-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WEED-290">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> In 2021, Trudeau announced the creation of a national <a href="/wiki/Child_care_in_Canada" title="Child care in Canada">child care</a> plan with the intention of reducing <a href="/wiki/Day_care" class="mw-redirect" title="Day care">day care</a> fees for parents down to $10 a day per child within five years.<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His environmental policy included introducing new commitments to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 30% before 2030, and to achieve net-zero emissions by 2050.<sup id="cite_ref-LucasPowers_292-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LucasPowers-292">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> His main tool for reaching this target is a federal <a href="/wiki/Carbon_pricing_in_Canada" title="Carbon pricing in Canada">carbon pricing</a> policy.<sup id="cite_ref-iec_293-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iec-293">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau's parliament also adopted legislation for marine conservation,<sup id="cite_ref-Lake_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lake-294">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup> banning six common <a href="/wiki/Single-use_plastic" class="mw-redirect" title="Single-use plastic">single-use plastic</a> products,<sup id="cite_ref-Hypocrisy_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hypocrisy-295">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> and strengthening environmental impact assessments.<sup id="cite_ref-JoshKElliott_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JoshKElliott-296">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau pledged to ban single use plastic in 2019. In the year 2022 his government announced a ban on producing and importing single use plastic from December 2022. The sale of those items will be banned from December 2023 and the export from 2025.<sup id="cite_ref-297" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-297">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> However, Trudeau is in favour of oil and gas pipelines to bring Canadian fossil fuel resources to foreign markets.<sup id="cite_ref-Swooning_298-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Swooning-298">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Justin_Trudeau_government" title="Foreign policy of the Justin Trudeau government">Foreign policy of the Justin Trudeau government</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Justin_Trudeau" title="List of international prime ministerial trips made by Justin Trudeau">List of international prime ministerial trips made by Justin Trudeau</a></div>
<p>In 2015, Trudeau  told the New York Times Magazine that Canada could be the “first <a href="/wiki/Postnationalism" title="Postnationalism">postnational</a> state".<sup id="cite_ref-299" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-299">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_(44348052040).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg/220px-Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg/330px-Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg/440px-Cumbre_de_l%C3%ADderes_del_G20_%2844348052040%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2264" data-file-height="1543" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau with US president <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a> and Mexican president <a href="/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto" title="Enrique Peña Nieto">Enrique Peña Nieto</a>, after signing the <a href="/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Mexico%E2%80%93Canada_Agreement" title="United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement">CUSMA</a>, 2018.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Trudeau enjoyed good relations with the "like-minded" <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">United States president</a> <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-300" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-300">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup> despite Trudeau's support for the <a href="/wiki/Keystone_Pipeline" title="Keystone Pipeline">Keystone Pipeline</a> which was rejected by the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic</a> president.<sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau's first foreign policy challenges included follow-through on his campaign promise to withdraw Canadian air support from the <a href="/wiki/Syrian_civil_war" title="Syrian civil war">Syrian civil war</a><sup id="cite_ref-Iraq/Syria_trainers,_aid_302-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Iraq/Syria_trainers,_aid-302">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> and to welcome 25,000 <a href="/wiki/Syrian_Refugees" class="mw-redirect" title="Syrian Refugees">Syrian war refugees</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-revisedgoal_303-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-revisedgoal-303">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup>
When Donald Trump became president, <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada-US relations</a> deteriorated. The Trump administration forced the renegotiation of <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">NAFTA</a> to create the <a href="/wiki/USMCA" class="mw-redirect" title="USMCA">CUSMA</a>, in which Canada made significant concessions in allowing increased imports of American <a href="/wiki/Milk" title="Milk">milk</a>, weakening Canada's dairy <a href="/wiki/Supply_management_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Supply management (Canada)">supply management</a> system.<sup id="cite_ref-304" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-304">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Canada_ratification_305-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Canada_ratification-305">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> Donald Trump also implemented <a href="/wiki/Trump_tariffs" title="Trump tariffs">tariffs</a> on Canadian <a href="/wiki/Steel" title="Steel">steel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aluminium" title="Aluminium">aluminium</a>, to which Trudeau retaliated by imposing tariffs on American steel, aluminium and a variety of other American products.<sup id="cite_ref-Wolfe229_306-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wolfe229-306">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau,_Volodymyr_Zelensky_(2022-05-08)_06.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg/220px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg/330px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg/440px-Justin_Trudeau%2C_Volodymyr_Zelensky_%282022-05-08%29_06.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3006" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>Amid the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Ukrainian_War" title="Russo-Ukrainian War">Russo-Ukrainian War</a>, Canadian Prime Minister Justin Trudeau visited <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kyiv</a> and met President <a href="/wiki/Volodymyr_Zelenskyy" title="Volodymyr Zelenskyy">Volodymyr Zelenskyy</a> on May 8, 2022</figcaption></figure>
<p>Canada's relationship with <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> also deteriorated during Justin Trudeau's time as Prime Minister. The turmoil led to the <a href="/wiki/Arrest_of_Meng_Wanzhou" class="mw-redirect" title="Arrest of Meng Wanzhou">arrest of Meng Wanzhou</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Vancouver_International_Airport" title="Vancouver International Airport">Vancouver International Airport</a> in December 2018 at the behest of the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-307" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-307">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Detention_of_Michael_Spavor_and_Michael_Kovrig" title="Detention of Michael Spavor and Michael Kovrig">arrest of Michael Spavor and Michael Kovrig</a> in China 12 days later.<sup id="cite_ref-308" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-308">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup> As these three individuals were released at the exact same time in September 2021, many observers speculated they were exchanged as part of a deal between the United States and China.<sup id="cite_ref-309" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-309">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a similar fashion, Canada's relationship with Saudi Arabia was also put under strain, as human rights groups called on Trudeau to stop selling military equipment to that country under a deal struck by the Harper government. In 2018, Saudi Arabia recalled its Canadian ambassador and froze trade with the country in response to Canada's call for the Saudis to release opposition blogger <a href="/wiki/Raif_Badawi" title="Raif Badawi">Raif Badawi</a>. However, in 2019, Canada doubled its weapons sales to Saudi Arabia, despite a "moratorium on export permits following the <a href="/wiki/Killing_of_Jamal_Khashoggi" class="mw-redirect" title="Killing of Jamal Khashoggi">killing of the Saudi journalist Jamal Khashoggi</a> and mounting civilian deaths from the <a href="/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen" class="mw-redirect" title="Saudi Arabian-led intervention in Yemen">Saudi Arabian-led intervention in Yemen</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-:1_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-310">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2023, Trudeau said that the government of Canada had "credible intelligence" that the government of India was involved in the killing of a Canadian citizen, <a href="/wiki/Hardeep_Singh_Nijjar" title="Hardeep Singh Nijjar">Hardeep Singh Nijjar</a>, outside a <a href="/wiki/Sikh_temple" class="mw-redirect" title="Sikh temple">Sikh temple</a> in <a href="/wiki/Surrey,_British_Columbia" title="Surrey, British Columbia">Surrey</a>. This episode caused a rapid deterioration of <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93India_relations" title="Canada–India relations">Canada–India relations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-311" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-311">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2020, Canada lost its bid to join the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council" title="United Nations Security Council">United Nations Security Council</a>. This was the second time Canada had failed an attempt to join the Security Council, the first time being in 2009 under Prime Minister Stephen Harper.<sup id="cite_ref-312" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-312">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Trudeau_family" title="Trudeau family">Trudeau family</a></div>
<p>Trudeau first met <a href="/wiki/Sophie_Gr%C3%A9goire" class="mw-redirect" title="Sophie Grégoire">Sophie Grégoire</a> when they were both children growing up in Montreal; Grégoire was a classmate and childhood friend of Trudeau's youngest brother, Michel.<sup id="cite_ref-GM_Family_Album_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GM_Family_Album-313">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> They reconnected as adults in June 2003, when Grégoire, by then a Quebec television personality, was assigned as Trudeau's co-host for a charity ball; they began dating several months later. Trudeau and Grégoire became engaged in October 2004 and married on May 28, 2005, in a ceremony at Montreal's Sainte-Madeleine d'Outremont Church.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc2005_314-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc2005-314">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> They have three children: a boy, Xavier, born in 2007, a girl, Ella-Grace, born in 2009, and a boy, Hadrien, born in 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-315" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-315">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg/220px-Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg/330px-Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg/440px-Justin_and_Sophie_Trudeau_-_Global_Citizen_Festival_Hamburg_08.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3342" data-file-height="2134" /></a><figcaption>Trudeau with his wife <a href="/wiki/Sophie_Gr%C3%A9goire" class="mw-redirect" title="Sophie Grégoire">Sophie Grégoire</a> at the 2017 <a href="/wiki/Global_Citizen_Festival" title="Global Citizen Festival">Global Citizen Festival</a> in Hamburg on the eve of the <a href="/wiki/2017_G20_Hamburg_summit" title="2017 G20 Hamburg summit">G20 Hamburg summit</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In June 2013, two months after Trudeau became the leader of the Liberal Party, the couple sold their home in the <a href="/wiki/C%C3%B4te-des-Neiges" title="Côte-des-Neiges">Côte-des-Neiges</a> neighbourhood of Montreal.<sup id="cite_ref-2013_Move_316-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2013_Move-316">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> They began living in a rented home in Ottawa's <a href="/wiki/Rockcliffe_Park" title="Rockcliffe Park">Rockcliffe Park</a>, the neighbourhood near where Trudeau resided as a child during his father's time as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-2013_Move_316-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2013_Move-316">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On August 18, 2014, an intruder broke into the house while Grégoire and the couple's three children were sleeping and left a threatening note; however, nothing was stolen and there was no damage to the property. Following the incident, Trudeau, who was in <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a> at the time of the break-in, stated his intention to inquire with the Royal Canadian Mounted Police about his home security.<sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> After his 2015 electoral victory, Trudeau opted to live at <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Cottage" title="Rideau Cottage">Rideau Cottage</a>, on the grounds of <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Hall" title="Rideau Hall">Rideau Hall</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rideau_Cottage_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rideau_Cottage-318">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On August 2, 2023, Trudeau announced he and Grégoire had separated.<sup id="cite_ref-319" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-319">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> On August 21, Trudeau said he was focusing on his children and the future.<sup id="cite_ref-320" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-320">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<p>Trudeau's father was a devout <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a><sup id="cite_ref-Memoir_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Memoir-321">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup> and his mother converted from <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church_of_Canada" title="Anglican Church of Canada">Anglicanism</a> to Catholicism just before their wedding.<sup id="cite_ref-322" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-322">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau himself became a <a href="/wiki/Lapsed_Catholic" title="Lapsed Catholic">lapsed Catholic</a> at age 18, as he felt that much of his day-to-day life was not addressed by the formality and structure of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-Ottawa_Citizen1_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ottawa_Citizen1-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Longhurst_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longhurst-323">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau described his faith during this period as "like so many Catholics across this country, I said, 'OK, I'm Catholic, I'm of faith, but I'm just not really going to go to church. Maybe on Easter, maybe <a href="/wiki/Midnight_Mass" title="Midnight Mass">midnight Mass</a> at Christmas.<span style="padding-right:.15em;">'</span>"<sup id="cite_ref-Ottawa_Citizen1_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ottawa_Citizen1-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Longhurst_323-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longhurst-323">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> After the death of his brother Michel in 1998, Trudeau was persuaded by a friend to participate in an <a href="/wiki/Alpha_course" title="Alpha course">Alpha course</a>, during which he regained his faith.<sup id="cite_ref-Ottawa_Citizen1_53-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ottawa_Citizen1-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Longhurst_323-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longhurst-323">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> In 2011, Trudeau stated, "My own personal faith is an extremely important part of who I am and the values that I try to lead with."<sup id="cite_ref-324" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-324">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Further_reading">Further reading</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/List_of_books_about_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="List of books about prime ministers of Canada">List of books about prime ministers of Canada</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1054258005"><div class="refbegin" style="">
<ul><li>Biscahie, Th. "Beyond the mosaic: Justin Trudeau and the postnational chimera." <i>London Journal of Canadian Studies</i> 34.3 (2019): 22-42. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10094281/1/03%20LJCS%20Vol%2034%202397-0605_Chapter_2.pdf">online</a></li></ul>
<ul><li>Bosworth, Yulia. "The 'bad' French of Justin Trudeau: When language, ideology, and politics collide." <i>American Review of Canadian Studies</i> 49.1 (2019): 5-24. <a rel="nofollow" class="external free" href="https://doi.org/10.1080/02722011.2019.1570954">https://doi.org/10.1080/02722011.2019.1570954</a></li>
<li>Clarke, Harold D., et al. "Like father, like son: Justin Trudeau and valence voting in Canada’s 2015 federal election." <i>PS: Political Science &amp; Politics</i> 50.3 (2017): 701-707. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://ore.exeter.ac.uk/repository/bitstream/handle/10871/28588/LikeFatherLikeSon-preprint.pdf?sequence=1">online</a></li>
<li>Coulon, Jocelyn. <i>Canada is Not Back: How Justin Trudeau is in over his head on foreign policy</i> (James Lorimer &amp; Company, 2019) <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=xm6ZDwAAQBAJ&amp;dq=%22Justin+Trudeau%22&amp;pg=PA3">online</a>.</li>
<li>Engler, Yves. <i>House of Mirrors: Justin Trudeau's Foreign Policy</i> (2020) <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.amazon.com/House-Mirrors-Justin-Trudeaus-Foreign/dp/1551647494/">excerpt</a></li>
<li>Findlay, Tammy. "Intersectionalities of Opportunism: Justin Trudeau and the Politics of 'Diversity'." <i>International Journal of Canadian Studies</i> 60 (2022): 40-59. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.utpjournals.press/doi/pdf/10.3138/ijcs.60.x.40">online</a></li>
<li>Hillmer, Norman, and Philippe Lagassé, eds. <i>Justin Trudeau and Canadian foreign policy</i> (Springer, 2018) <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=C5ZaDwAAQBAJ&amp;dq=Justin+Trudeau+and+Canadian+Foreign+Policy+Norman+Hillmer,+Philippe+Lagass%C3%A9+0+/+&amp;pg=PR6">online</a>.</li>
<li>Kasztenna, Katarzyna Kasia. "Framing the pandemic in the political discourse of Justin Trudeau and Donald Trump: A reconnaissance." <i>Working papers in Applied Linguistics and Linguistics at York</i> 2 (2022): 29-50. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://wally.journals.yorku.ca/index.php/default/article/download/14/21">online</a></li>
<li>Lalancette, Mireille, and Vincent Raynauld. "The power of political image: Justin Trudeau, Instagram, and celebrity politics." <i>American behavioral scientist</i> 3#7 (2019): 888-924. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Mireille-Lalancette/publication/326637126_Justin_Trudeau_and_the_play_of_celebrity_in_the_2015_Canadian_federal_election_campaign/links/5c66ed534585156b57ffe2a2/Justin-Trudeau-and-the-play-of-celebrity-in-the-2015-Canadian-federal-election-campaign.pdf">online</a></li>
<li>Lalancette, Mireille, and Tamara A. Small. " 'Justin Trudeau—I Don’t Know Her': An Analysis of Leadership Memes of Justin Trudeau." <i>Canadian Journal of Communication</i> 45.2 (2020): 305-325. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.utpjournals.press/doi/pdf/10.22230/cjc.2020v45n2a3445">online</a></li>
<li>Lees-Marshment, Jennifer, and Salma Malik. "Political Branding in a Crisis and the Shifting Strategies of the Trudeau 2021 Campaign." in <i>Political Marketing in the 2021 Canadian Federal Election</i> (Cham: Springer International Publishing, 2023) pp. 9-23.</li>
<li>Lim, Preston. "Sino-Canadian relations in the age of Justin Trudeau." <i>Canadian Foreign Policy Journal</i> 26.1 (2020): 25-40. <a rel="nofollow" class="external free" href="https://doi.org/10.1080/11926422.2019.1641118">https://doi.org/10.1080/11926422.2019.1641118</a></li>
<li>Marland, Alex. "The brand image of Canadian Prime Minister Justin Trudeau in international context." <i>Canadian Foreign Policy Journal</i> 24.2 (2018): 139-144. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/download/56541643/Justin_Trudeau_brand_international_Marland_2018.pdf">online</a></li>
<li>Remillard, Chaseten, Lindsey M. Bertrand, and Alina Fisher. "The visually viral prime minister: Justin Trudeau, selfies, and Instagram." in <i>Power Shift? Political Leadership and Social Media</i> (Routledge, 2019) pp. 63-75. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Alina-Fisher/publication/338951228_The_Visually_Viral_Prime_Minister_Justin_Trudeau_Selfies_and_Instagram/links/5e344e95299bf1cdb90255f8/The-Visually-Viral-Prime-Minister-Justin-Trudeau-Selfies-and-Instagram.pdf">online</a></li>
<li>Wherry, Aaron. <i>Promise and Peril: Justin Trudeau in Power</i> (HarperCollins, 2019). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.amazon.com/Promise-Peril-Justin-Trudeau-Power/dp/1443458279/">excerpt</a></li>
<li>Young, Huguette. <i>Justin Trudeau: The Natural Heir</i> (Dundurn, 2016) a major biography. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=S6vrCgAAQBAJ&amp;dq=%22Justin+Trudeau%22&amp;pg=PP1">online</a></li></ul>

<div class="navbox-styles"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1061467846"></div><div role="navigation" class="navbox" aria-labelledby="Pierre_Trudeau" style="padding:3px"><table class="nowraplinks hlist mw-collapsible autocollapse navbox-inner" style="border-spacing:0;background:transparent;color:inherit"><tbody><tr><th scope="col" class="navbox-title" colspan="3"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Pierre_Trudeau" title="Template:Pierre Trudeau"><abbr title="View this template" style=";;background:none transparent;border:none;box-shadow:none;padding:0;">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Pierre_Trudeau" title="Template talk:Pierre Trudeau"><abbr title="Discuss this template" style=";;background:none transparent;border:none;box-shadow:none;padding:0;">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Pierre_Trudeau" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Pierre Trudeau"><abbr title="Edit this template" style=";;background:none transparent;border:none;box-shadow:none;padding:0;">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div><div id="Pierre_Trudeau" style="font-size:114%;margin:0 4em"><a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a></div></th></tr><tr><td class="navbox-abovebelow" colspan="3"><div>
<ul><li>October 18, 1919 – September 28, 2000<br /><a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="List of prime ministers of Canada">15th</a> <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister of Canada</a> (1968–1979, 1980–1984)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Leader of the Liberal Party of Canada</a> (1968–1984)</li>
